Online Supplementary Appendix B to
“Estimation and Inference for Three-Dimensional Panel Data Models”

Abstract
Hierarchical panel data models have recently garnered significant attention. This study contributes to the relevant literature by introducing a novel three-dimensional (3D) hierarchical panel data model, which integrates panel regression with three sets of latent factor structures: one set of global factors and two sets of local factors. Instead of aggregating latent factors from various nodes, as seen in the literature of distributed principal component analysis (PCA), we propose an estimation approach capable of recovering the parameters of interest and disentangling latent factors at different levels and across different dimensions. We establish an asymptotic theory and provide a bootstrap procedure to obtain inference for the parameters of interest while accommodating various types of cross-sectional dependence and time series autocorrelation. Finally, we demonstrate the applicability of our framework by examining productivity convergence in manufacturing industries worldwide. Keywords : Asymptotic Theory, Bias Correction, Dependent Wild Bootstrap, Hierarchical Model JEL classification : C23, O10, L60



Estimation and Inference for

Three-Dimensional Panel Data Models

1
Introduction

In the past three decades or so, panel data research has undergone significant advancements, leveraging its capacity to extract richer insights from both cross-sectional and time dimensions. Within this domain, a notable strand of research (
Bai, 2009
;
Fan et al., 2016
) has garnered increasing attention, with a particular focus on addressing cross-sectional dependence (CSD) in large
N
and
T
settings by accounting for correlations among observations across different entities within a panel. Specifically, strong CSD is often addressed through a factor structure approach involving the estimation of a low-rank representation, while weak CSD poses inference challenges related to estimating the asymptotic covariance matrix. Additionally, accounting for time series autocorrelation (TSA) is essential when analyzing data with a temporal dimension. To tackle both CSD and TSA jointly, various methods have been proposed in the relevant literature. For instance,
Gonçalves (2011)
suggests using the moving block bootstrap method,
Bai et al. (2020)
employ a thresholding method in conjunction with a heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation-consistent (HAC) covariance matrix estimator, and
Gao et al. (2023)
propose a dependent wild bootstrap procedure by extending the time series approach of
Shao (2010)
to a two–dimensional panel data framework.

Recent developments in panel data research, especially in contexts where observations exhibit hierarchical structures, have led to the emergence of the relevant literature on hierarchical panel data models (
Matyas et al., 2017
;
Kapetanios et al., 2021
;
Chen et al., 2022
;
Zhang et al., 2023
;
Jin et al., 2024a
;
Jin et al., 2024b
). This evolution reflects the recognition of the necessity to extend beyond traditional panel data models to capture dependencies at multiple levels of aggregation, such as individuals nested within groups or regions nested within countries. Consequently, the literature on hierarchical panel data models builds upon and extends methodologies developed in the cross-sectional dependence literature to accommodate these hierarchical structures, ultimately enabling researchers to more accurately model and analyze complex panel datasets.

Another pertinent literature is regression with incidental parameters, a concept pioneered by
Neyman and Scott (1948)
.
Lancaster (2000)
commends their seminal paper as a remarkable contribution, widely acknowledged for its influence within the statistical community. To see the challenges posed by incidental parameters in the context of 3 dimensional (3D) panels, we present the following table outlining the potential incidental parameters across different data structures:

Data Structure

Possible Incidental Parameters

2 Dimensional Panel

(1).
\alpha_{i}
(Individual effects); (2).
g_{t}
(Time effects);

(3).
\alpha_{i}+g_{t}
(Two way effects); (4). No effects

3 Dimensional Panel

64 possible specifications (
Lu et al., 2021
)

While a 3D panel involves only one additional layer, it significantly increases complexity. To the best of our knowledge, no prior studies have attempted to integrate all potential specifications of incidental parameters into a single regression model. Hence, a unified framework is needed. Fortunately, incidental parameters can be integrated using a factor structure. For example, all four conceivable scenarios of a 2D panel can fit within a structure such as
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{g}_{t}
. This approach offers valuable insights into addressing the challenge of 3D panel data modeling.

With the above challenges in mind, the primary objective of this study is to contribute to the literature on hierarchical panel data models by introducing a panel data regression model with three sets of latent factor structures: one set of global factors and two sets of local factors. Compared with pure factor models, this model is capable of estimating the coefficient of a particular observed independent variable while accounting for both commonalities and individual differences across entities. In addition, in comparison with panel data regression models with only one or two sets of factors, the proposed model and estimation theory is capable to model dependencies that exist at multiple levels of aggregation. This model finds applications in various contexts. For example, in the context of modeling economic convergence, the latent global factors represent economic events affecting the growth of all countries and industries, while the two local factors represent events affecting specific countries or industries, respectively. In the context of modeling international trade, the latent global factors represent factors affecting the trade of all countries, while the two local factors represent events affecting importing or exporting countries, respectively.

To better understand the 3D factor structure, suppose that we have two groups of individuals:

\displaystyle i\in[L]\quad\text{and}\quad j\in[N],

(1.1)

where
L
and
N
are two positive integers, and
[S]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,S\}
for any positive integer
S
. In the literature of modeling empirical growth (e.g.,
Rodrik, 2013
),
i
represents industries, and
j
denotes countries; in the context of bilateral trade (e.g.,
Chen et al., 2022
),
i
and
j
respectively represent importing countries and exporting countries; in the stochastic actor-oriented models (e.g.,
Koskinen and Snijders, 2023
),
i
refers to social groups and
j
refers to individuals within each group; etc. In addition, we let
y_{ijt}
denote a set of outcome variables:

\displaystyle\{y_{ijt}\,|\,(i,j,t)\in[L]\times[N]\times[T]\}

(1.2)

which is indexed by both
i
and
j
, and also varies over each time period
t
. The definition of
y_{ijt}
should be self-evident in the aforementioned studies, so we omit these details here. Throughout this paper, we assume that each layer of information is influenced by distinct factors, enabling us to extract essential insights through careful data processing. Our approach diverges in that we aim to disentangle factors layer by layer and across different blocks within each layer, rather than consolidating factors from disparate nodes.

For a clearer grasp of the 3D factor structure, we visually represent the three sets of latent factors (
\mathbf{f}_{t}
,
\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}
) in Figure
1
, utilizing empirical growth data as a demonstration.

i\in[L]
j\in[N]
\mathbf{f}_{t}
y_{ijt}
\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}
\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}

Industries

Countries

Global factors

Figure 1:
Different Factors of the Hierarchy

Specifically,
\mathbf{f}_{t}
,
\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}
represent
\ell\times 1
,
\ell_{i}^{\circ}\times 1
, and
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}\times 1
vectors respectively, denoting the global, country-specific, and industry-specific factors or shocks driving
y_{ijt}
. Here,
\ell
,
\ell_{i}^{\circ}
, and
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}
are non-negative fixed integers, subject to the constraint:

\displaystyle 0\leq\min_{i,j}(\ell+\ell_{i}^{\circ}+\ell_{j}^{\bullet})\leq\max_{i,j}(\ell+\ell_{i}^{\circ}+\ell_{j}^{\bullet})\leq c,

(1.3)

where
c
is a fixed positive constant. This condition implies that
\ell
,
\ell_{i}^{\circ}
, and
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}
can potentially be 0. In an extreme scenario, if
y_{ijt}
behaves entirely randomly like an idiosyncratic error, then
\ell=\ell_{i}^{\circ}=\ell_{j}^{\bullet}=0
for all
(i,j)
pairs. Figure
1
illustrates that
f_{t}
influences every industry and country, while each country-specific (or industry-specific) shock can also impact a specific subset of countries or industries. Mathematically, for all
t\in[T]
, Figure 1 reveals the following mapping:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{11}^{\top}&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{11}^{\circ\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{0}&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{11}^{\bullet\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{0}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{L1}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{L1}^{\circ\top}&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{L1}^{\bullet\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{0}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&&&\vdots&\\
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1N}^{\top}&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1N}^{\circ\top}&\cdots&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1N}^{\bullet\top}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{LN}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{LN}^{\circ\top}&\mathbf{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{LN}^{\bullet\top}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{f}_{t}\\
\mathbf{f}_{1t}^{\circ}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{f}_{Lt}^{\circ}\\
\mathbf{f}_{1t}^{\bullet}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{f}_{Nt}^{\bullet}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}y_{11t}\\
\vdots\\
y_{L1t}\\
\vdots\\
y_{1Nt}\\
\vdots\\
y_{LNt}\end{pmatrix},

(1.4)

where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ}
, and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet}
are factor loadings indicating how each factor influences
y_{ijt}
at each time period
t
. Figure
1
, along with the restriction (
1.3
) and the mapping (
1.4
), together entail the following observations:

1.

Figure
1
, the restriction (
1.3
), and the mapping (
1.4
) encompass all possible combinations of incidental parameters.

2.

The global shock
\mathbf{f}_{t}
(if exists) affects all countries and industries, while the country and industry shocks (
\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}
) respectively impact a specific subset of countries or industries.

3.

From the a signal-to-noise ratio point of view,
\mathbf{f}_{t}
can be recovered by aggregating all available information, whereas
\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}
can be identified block by block.

4.

The dimensions
i
and
j
exhibit symmetry, despite potential differences in the values of
L
and
N
.

Finally, to accommodate potential endogeneity, we posit that the unobservable factors exert influence on regressors, subject to less restrictive conditions (refer to Remark
1
and Assumption
1
for more details). Further elaboration on this aspect will be provided when detailing the model setup and associated conditions in Section
2
. Our objective is twofold: to estimate the coefficients of these endogenous regressors and to discern factors across various levels or blocks of the hierarchy.

Concluding this section, we summarize our contributions as follows. In addition to the introduction of a panel data regression model featuring three sets of latent factor structures, this study also provides additional contributions in the following areas:

1.

We develop an estimation method to recover the parameters of interest and unobservable factors across levels and blocks of the hierarchy, and establish its asymptotic properties. The unified hierarchy structure in a 3D framework covers the total 64 possibilities of incidental parameters which have been the centre of a wide range of applications since
Neyman and Scott (1948)
.

2.

The main model and its estimation method discussed in Section 2 below are developed for the case where both cross–sectional dependence and serial correlation are allowed for the error terms. By contrast, the existing literature (see,
Jin et al. (2024b)
, for example) focuses on the case where the error components are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.).

3.

We propose a bootstrap procedure to obtain inferences for the parameters of interest, accommodating various types of cross–sectional dependence (CSD) and time series autocorrelation (TSA) within the data generating process of the hierarchy.

4.

To validate our theoretical findings, we conduct extensive simulations and analyze real data examples. In the empirical study, we specifically utilize data from manufacturing industries at the ISIC two-digit level to examine the twin hypotheses of conditional and unconditional convergence for manufacturing industries across countries.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
presents our model and methodology. An asymptotic theory is established accordingly for each step involved in different estimation procedures. Section
3
conducts extensive numerical studies to examine the theoretical findings. Specifically, Section
3
examines the theoretical findings using extensive simulations, while Section
4
uses a set of data from manufacturing industries at the ISIC two-digit level to examine the twin hypotheses of conditional and unconditional-convergence for manufacturing industries across countries. Section
5
concludes. In the online supplementary Appendices A and B, Appendix A lists the necessary tables and pictures for Section 4. Appendix
B.1
provides two detailed numerical implementations; Appendix
B.2
proposes a Jackknife based bias correction method; Appendix
B.3
includes the necessary preliminary lemmas; Appendix
B.4
presents the detailed proofs; some additional estimation results of the empirical study are reported in Appendix B.

Before proceeding further, it is convenient to introduce some notations that will be repeatedly used throughout the article. Vectors and matrices are always written in bold font. For a matrix
\mathbf{A}
,
\|\mathbf{A}\|
and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
denote the Frobenius norm and the spectral norm of
\mathbf{A}
, respectively, and
\mathbf{A}^{\top}
stands for the transpose of
\mathbf{A}
. Provided that
\mathbf{A}
has full column rank, let
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{A}}
with
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}
. For two block wise matrices
\mathbf{A}=\{\mathbf{a}_{ij}\}
and
\mathbf{B}=\{\mathbf{b}_{ij}\}
, provided
\mathbf{a}_{ij}\mathbf{b}_{ij}
is well defined, we let
\circ
define the block wise Hadamard product, i.e.,
\mathbf{A}\circ\mathbf{B}=\{\mathbf{a}_{ij}\mathbf{b}_{ij}\}
. For each
\mathbf{a}_{ij}
, let
\sum_{i,j}\mathbf{a}_{ij}=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbf{a}_{ij}
.
Also, we let
\diag\{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\}
return a diagonal matrix with
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
on the main diagonal. For a vector
\mathbf{b}
, let
\|\mathbf{b}\|_{1}
define the
L^{1}
norm. For two scalars
m
and
n
,
m\wedge n=\min\{m,n\}
,
m\vee n=\max\{m,n\}
. For two random variables
a
and
b
,
a\asymp b
stands for
a=O_{P}(b)
and
b=O_{P}(a)
.
I(\cdot)
represents the conventional indicator function, and “
\to_{P}
” and “
\to_{D}
” stand for convergence in probability and convergence in distribution, respectively.
E^{*}
and
\text{Pr}^{*}
stand for the expectation and probability induced by the bootstrap procedure.

2
Model, Assumptions and Estimation Method

In this section, we will introduce our panel data regression model featuring three sets of latent factor structures, outline a method for estimating the model, and establish its asymptotic properties. Before delving into the specifics of our model, we wish to emphasize two crucial points. Firstly, our main focus is on regressing
y_{ijt}
on a
d\times 1
vector
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
, where
d
is fixed. Secondly, as discussed in the introduction, we hypothesize that the unobservable factors impact the regressors to address potential endogeneity. With this consideration, our panel data regression model with three sets of latent factor structures is formulated as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}^{\top}\\
0&\mathbf{I}_{d}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}y_{ijt}\\
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\top}\\
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{f}_{t}+\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ\top}\\
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\circ\top}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}+\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet\top}\\
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\bullet\top}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}+\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{ijt}\\
\mathbf{v}_{ijt}\end{pmatrix},

(2.1)

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
and every quantity on the right hand side of (
2.1
) are unknown, and
(i,j,t)\in[L]\times[N]\times[T]
with
(L,N,T)
can all be large.

Our goal is to infer
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
, and to recover the spaces spanned by the three sets of unobservable factors. For the time being, we assume that
\ell
,
\ell_{i}^{\circ}
’s and
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}
’s are known, and we will address their estimation in Section
2.4
. The setup connects with the existing works, such as
Ando and Bai (2017)
,
Kapetanios et al. (2021)
and
Jin et al. (2024a)
, by addressing many challenges of the relevant literature within one framework. Meanwhile, it is worth mentioning that the paper by
Jin et al. (2024b)
has an almost identical setup as our model (
2.1
), and they offer a set of tests on the heterogeneous coefficients which are driven by the independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) error components attached to the coefficients. In this paper, we pay particular attention to the case where the random errors of the main model are allowed to be both cross–sectionally dependent and serially correlated.

We now rewrite the main equation (i.e., the upper equation) of (
2.1
) in vector form as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}+\mathbf{F}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}+\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ}+\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij\centerdot},

(2.2)

where
\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}=(y_{ij1},\ldots,y_{ijT})^{\top}
,
\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}=(\mathbf{x}_{ij1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{ijT})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij\centerdot}=(\varepsilon_{ij1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{ijT})^{\top}
,

\mathbf{F}=(\mathbf{f}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{f}_{T})^{\top}
,
\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}=(\mathbf{f}_{i1}^{\circ},\ldots,\mathbf{f}_{iT}^{\circ})^{\top}
, and
\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}=(\mathbf{f}_{j1}^{\bullet},\ldots,\mathbf{f}_{jT}^{\bullet})^{\top}
.

Remark 1
.

We refrain from expressing
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
in matrix form at this stage, as we aim to minimize constraints on
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\circ}
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\bullet}
as much as possible. These parameters may even exhibit characteristics akin to weak factor loadings, as observed in
Uematsu and Yamagata (2023)
. If the focus is about the structure of
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
only, we refer the interested reader to
Jin et al. (2024a)
for a comprehensive investigation on the factor structure without regressors.

According to (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), it is reasonable to assume that for
\forall(i,j)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}=(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ},\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet})

(2.3)

has full column rank. Otherwise, one can always reorganize the unobservable factors to achieve a representation with full column rank.

Remark 2
.

If
\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}
is observable, we can immediately rewrite (
2.2
) as

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}}\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}=\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}+\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij\centerdot},

(2.4)

which enables consistent estimation for
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
using the OLS method. For the case with unobservable
\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}
, a very intuitive idea following a typical two dimensional panel data approach (such as
Ando and Bai, 2017
) is to consider an objective function in the following form:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij})^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{C}_{ij}}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij}),

(2.5)

where each
\mathbf{C}_{ij}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ},\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet})
is a generic matrix satisfying
\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\top}\mathbf{C}_{ij}=\mathbf{I}_{\ell+\ell_{i}^{\circ}+\ell_{i}^{\bullet}}
. One would hope to obtain the estimates of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
and
\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}
by combining the quantity of (
2.5
) over
(i,j)
. However, the restriction
\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\top}\mathbf{C}_{ij}=\mathbf{I}_{\ell+\ell_{i}^{\circ}+\ell_{i}^{\bullet}}
simply cannot be fulfilled practically. To illustrate this, suppose we consider a fixed
i
. Then
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ})
allow us to generate a set of
\mathbf{C}_{|i}^{\bullet}=\{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet}\}
. Once moving on to a different
i^{*}
,
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i^{*}}^{\circ})
will generate another set of
\mathbf{C}_{|i^{*}}^{\bullet}=\{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet}\}
. In general,
\mathbf{C}_{|i}^{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{C}_{|i^{*}}^{\bullet}
are not necessarily the same unless (1):
(L\vee N)\ll T
; and (2):
\{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}\}
and
\{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet}\}
are generated from two orthogonal spaces from the perspective of vector multiplication. However, it rules out the most common restriction, such as
L\asymp N\asymp T
.

To tackle the problem raised in Remark
2
, we put our thoughts in a nutshell below. For simplicity, suppose that as
T\rightarrow\infty

\displaystyle\max_{i,j}\left\|\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*\top}\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}-\mathbf{I}_{\ell+\ell_{i}^{\circ}+\ell_{j}^{\bullet}}\right\|=o_{P}(1),

(2.6)

which does not lose any generality, as it is only a matter of rotating matrices in view of the factor structure admitting the following expression:

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}+\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ}+\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet}=\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}

(2.7)

in which
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}=(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\top},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ\top},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet\top})^{\top}
. Lemme
B.2
of the online Appendix A shows the feasibility of (
2.6
) when taking
\max
over
(i,j)
. By (
2.6
), simple algebra shows that

\displaystyle\max_{i,j}\left\|\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}}-\left(\mathbf{I}_{T}-\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{\top}-\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ\top}-\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet\top}\right)\right\|=o_{P}(1),

(2.8)

in which the representation
\mathbf{I}_{T}-\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{\top}-\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ\top}-\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet\top}
automatically separates
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}
, and also serves as a projection matrix asymptotically. Thus, (
2.8
) sheds light on how to recover
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}
within a single framework. We are now ready to proceed.

2.1
Estimation Procedure

We start by introducing a set of new symbols. Let
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet}
be some generic matrices each sharing the same dimensions with
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}
respectively. We define

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{\centerdot\centerdot}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\circ},\mathbf{C}^{\bullet})^{\top},\quad\mathbf{C}^{\circ}=(\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\circ},\ldots,\mathbf{C}_{L}^{\circ}),\quad\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}=(\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\bullet},\ldots,\mathbf{C}_{N}^{\bullet}).

(2.9)

Similar to
\mathbf{C}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}
, we define
\mathbf{F}^{\circ}=(\mathbf{F}_{1}^{\circ},\ldots,\mathbf{F}_{L}^{\circ})
and
\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}=(\mathbf{F}_{1}^{\bullet},\ldots,\mathbf{F}_{N}^{\bullet}).
Also, let

\mathbf{b}_{\centerdot\centerdot}=(\mathbf{b}_{11},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{1N},\ldots\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{LN})^{\top}

be a generic matrix with dimensions matching
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot\centerdot}=(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{11},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1N},\ldots\ldots,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{LN})^{\top}
.

Using these symbols, we introduce the following objective function:

\displaystyle Q(\mathbf{b}_{\centerdot\centerdot},\mathbf{C}_{\centerdot\centerdot})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{LNT}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij})^{\top}\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij}),

(2.10)

where
\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ})}+\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet})}
and the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{I}_{\ell},\quad\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ\top}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}=\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{i}^{\circ}},\quad\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet\top}\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet}=\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{j}^{\bullet}},\quad\mathbf{C}^{\top}(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ},\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet})=\mathbf{0}_{\ell\times(\ell_{i}^{\circ}+\ell_{i}^{\bullet})}.

(2.11)

Remark 3
.

We stress a few key points below.

1.

One may regard either
\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ})}
or
\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet})}
as a projection matrix to be used to recover the global factors with slightly over-specified number of factors. The fact is reflected in the following operation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{\dagger}\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}=(\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ})}+\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet})})(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ},\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet})=(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet})}\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ},\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ})}\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}),

where
\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ})}+\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{F},\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet})}
. By Assumption
1
.3, it is then easy to show that

\displaystyle\max_{i,j}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\|(\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{\dagger}\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*})-(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ},\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet})\|=o_{P}(1),

so a structure like
\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{\dagger}
only projects out the global factor, and keeps the local factors in the system as residuals. It reveals one may be able to recover global and local factors sequentially due to different signal strength.

2.

The first three conditions of (
2.11
) are standard in the literature. The last condition essentially requires
\mathbf{C}\perp\mathbf{C}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{C}\perp\mathbf{C}^{\bullet}
. Rather than labeling it as a constraint, it merely dictates the sequence of calculations when minimizing
Q(\mathbf{b}_{\centerdot\centerdot},\mathbf{C}_{\centerdot\centerdot})
.

Consider
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}
as an example. By (
2.11
), we can write

\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ})}+\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet})}=2\mathbf{I}_{T}-2\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{C}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet}},

so
\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger}
maintains a structure similar to that in (
2.8
). Minimizing
Q(\mathbf{b}_{\centerdot\centerdot},\mathbf{C}_{\centerdot\centerdot})
with respect to
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}
is equivalent to maximizing the following quantity:

\displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}}\tr\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij})^{\top}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ\top}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij})\right)

(2.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}}\tr\left(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ\top}\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{C}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij})(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\mathbf{b}_{ij})^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}\right),

where the equality holds because of
\mathbf{C}\perp\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ}
. Equation (
2.12
) implies that in order to obtain the estimates of
\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}
, one should project out the estimate of
\mathbf{F}
firstly, which is automatically taken into account in view of (
2.10
) and (
2.12
). Now, how to minimize global and local factors sequentially becomes even clearer.

3.

Additionally, since

\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger})\geq\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ})})+\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet})})=0,

we have
Q(\mathbf{b}_{\centerdot\centerdot},\mathbf{C}_{\centerdot\centerdot})\geq 0
to ensure the non-negativity of the objective function.

Having explained the above points, we outline our estimation procedure below.

Step 1
Perform the following optimization:

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot},\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{\centerdot\centerdot})=\argmin Q(\mathbf{b}_{\centerdot\centerdot},\mathbf{C}_{\centerdot\centerdot}),

(2.13)

where
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}=(\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{11},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{LN})^{\top}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}=(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\circ},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{L}^{\circ},\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{1}^{\bullet},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{N}^{\bullet})^{\top}
. Here, (
2.13
) can be decomposed as follows:

1.

\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}=(\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}^{\dagger}\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot},
where
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}^{\dagger}=2\mathbf{I}_{T}-2\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}}-\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\circ}}-\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j}^{\bullet}}
;

2.

\widehat{\mathbf{C}}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
, where
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}
includes the largest
\ell
eigenvalues of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\frac{1}{LNT}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij})(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij})^{\top};

3.

For
\forall i\in[L]
,
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\circ}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{i}^{\circ}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{\circ}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\circ}
, where
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{i}^{\circ}
includes the largest
\ell_{i}^{\circ}
eigenvalues of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{\circ}
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{\circ}=\frac{1}{NT}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij})(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij})^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}};

4.

For
\forall j\in[N]
,
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j}^{\bullet}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}^{\bullet}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j}^{\bullet}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j}^{\bullet}
, where
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_{j}^{\bullet}
includes the largest
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}
eigenvalues of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j}^{\bullet}
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j}^{\bullet}=\frac{1}{LT}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}}(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij})(\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij})^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}}.

Step 2
Finally, update the estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}=(\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot})^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}}\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot},

(2.14)

where
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}=(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\circ},\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j}^{\bullet})
.

The detailed numerical implementation in provided in Appendix
B.1
. In the next subsection, we propose the above estimation procedure, and establish the corresponding asymptotic properties.

2.2
Main Assumptions

In the following, we first provide some notation and mathematical symbols which will be repeatedly used in Assumptions and theoretical development of the paper. Key assumptions with their justifications will follow.

Notation
:

Regressors:
\mathbf{X}_{i\centerdot\centerdot}=(\mathbf{X}_{i1\centerdot},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{iN\centerdot})
and
\mathbf{X}_{\centerdot j\centerdot}=(\mathbf{X}_{1j\centerdot},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{Lj\centerdot})
;

Errors:
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\centerdot\centerdot\centerdot}=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{1\centerdot\centerdot},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{L\centerdot\centerdot})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i\centerdot\centerdot}=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i1\centerdot},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{iN\centerdot})
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\centerdot j\centerdot}=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{1j\centerdot},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{Lj\centerdot})
,
\mathbf{V}_{\centerdot\centerdot\centerdot}=(\mathbf{V}_{1\centerdot\centerdot},\cdots,\mathbf{V}_{L\centerdot\centerdot})^{\top}
,
\mathbf{V}_{i\centerdot\centerdot}=(\mathbf{V}_{i1\centerdot},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{iN\centerdot})
,
\mathbf{V}_{\centerdot j\centerdot}=(\mathbf{V}_{1j\centerdot},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{Lj\centerdot})
, and
\mathbf{V}_{ij\centerdot}=(\mathbf{v}_{ij1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{ijT})^{\top}
;

Loadings:
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{*}=(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\top},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\circ\top},\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\bullet\top})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}=(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\top},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ\top},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet\top})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\centerdot}^{\circ}=(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i1}^{\circ},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{iN}^{\circ})^{\top}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\centerdot j}^{\bullet}=(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1j}^{\bullet},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{Lj}^{\bullet})^{\top}
.

Assumption 1
.

1.

For the error components, we impose the following conditions:

(a)

\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\centerdot\centerdot\centerdot}\|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{LN}\vee\sqrt{T})
, and
\left(\frac{\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i\centerdot\centerdot}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{NT}}+\frac{\max_{j}\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\centerdot j\centerdot}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{LT}}\right)\cdot\log(L\vee N\vee T)=o_{P}(1)
;

(b)

|\frac{1}{LNT}\sum_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}|=O_{P}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{LN\wedge T}})
, and
\left|\frac{1}{LNT}\sum_{i,j}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}\right|=O_{P}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{LN\wedge T}})
’

(c)

\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{\centerdot\centerdot\centerdot}\|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{LN}\vee\sqrt{T})
,
\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{i\centerdot\centerdot}\|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{N}\vee\sqrt{T})
, and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{V}}_{\centerdot j\centerdot}\|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{L}\vee\sqrt{T})
, for
\forall(i,j)
.

2.

For the loadings, we impose the following conditions:

(a)

\frac{1}{LN}\sum_{i,j}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*\top}-E[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*\top}])=o_{P}(1)
in which

\displaystyle 0<\min_{i,j}\lambda_{\min}(E[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*\top}])\leq\max_{i,j}\lambda_{\max}(E[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*\top}])<\infty;

(b)

\max_{i}\|\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\centerdot}^{\circ\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\centerdot}^{\circ}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma},i}^{\circ}\|=o_{P}(1)
in which

\displaystyle 0<\min_{i}\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma},i}^{\circ})\leq\max_{i}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma},i}^{\circ})<\infty;

(c)

\max_{j}\|\frac{1}{L}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\centerdot j}^{\bullet\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\centerdot j}^{\bullet}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma},j}^{\bullet}\|=o_{P}(1)
in which

\displaystyle 0<\min_{j}\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma},j}^{\bullet})\leq\max_{j}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma},j}^{\bullet})<\infty.

3.

For the factors, we impose the following conditions:

(a)

\log(L\vee N\vee T)\cdot\max_{i,j}\|\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*\top}\mathbf{F}_{ij}^{*}-\mathbf{I}_{\ell+\ell_{i}^{\circ}+\ell_{j}^{\bullet}}\|=o_{P}(1)
;

(b)

\|\mathbf{F}^{\circ}\|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{L}\vee\sqrt{T})
and
\|\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}\|_{2}=O_{P}(\sqrt{N}\vee\sqrt{T})
.

4.

For the regressors, we impose the following conditions:

(a)

\max_{i}\frac{1}{NT}\|\mathbf{X}_{i\centerdot\centerdot}\|^{2}=O_{P}(1)
;

(b)

\max_{j}\frac{1}{LT}\|\mathbf{X}_{\centerdot j\centerdot}\|^{2}=O_{P}(1)
.

5.

Suppose that the following conditions are satisfied with probability approaching 1:

\inf_{\mathbf{C}_{\centerdot\centerdot}}\lambda_{\min}\left(\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{D}_{\centerdot\centerdot}\right)>0,

where
\mathbf{D}_{\centerdot\centerdot}=\diag\{\mathbf{D}_{11},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{LN}\}
,
\mathbf{D}_{ij,1}=\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}
,
\mathbf{D}_{ij,2}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}\otimes(\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot})
, and
\mathbf{D}_{ij}=\mathbf{D}_{ij,1}-\mathbf{D}_{ij,2}^{\top}(E[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\top}]\otimes\mathbf{I}_{T})^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{ij,2}
.

Assumption
1
imposes a set of regular conditions. All of the conditions involving
\max_{i}
,
\max_{j}
and
\max_{ij}
can be easily justified in the same fashion as in Lemma
B.2
in Appendix A below. However, as we cannot define some type of mixing conditions along with the dimension
i
or
j
, so we state them as they are via a set of high level assumptions.
Assumption
1
.1.(a) specifies the spectral norm orders of random errors, which can be satisfied by various examples discussed in the supplementary Appendix S.2 of
Moon and Weidner (2017)
. In particular, it can be justified by i.i.d.
\varepsilon_{ijt}
with zero mean and uniformly bounded fourth moment
E[\varepsilon_{ijt}^{4}]
. Following the arguments in Lemma S.2.1 of
Moon and Weidner (2017)
, we can demonstrate that Assumption
1
.1.(a) also holds for the MA(
\infty
) process:
\varepsilon_{ijt}=\sum_{\tau=0}^{\infty}a_{ij\tau}e_{ij,t-\tau}
, where
e_{ijt}
are i.i.d. with zero mean and uniformly bounded fourth moment, and the MA coefficients
{a_{ij\tau}}
satisfy certain uniform boundedness conditions.

Assumption
1
.1.(b) is met by strictly exogenous and i.i.d. or
\alpha
-mixing errors. Assumption
1
.2 regulates the behaviour of factor loadings, which can be justified by identically distributed factor loadings with weak cross–sectional dependence. Assumption
1
.3 imposes conditions on the factors, which can be easily satisfied. For instance, Assumption
1
.3.(a) can be met by
\mathbf{f}_{t}
,
\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}
that are independently generated from a multivariate standard normal distribution. In Assumption
1
.3.(b), the required orders of spectral norm of
\mathbf{F}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}
can be fulfilled by the i.i.d. or MA(
\infty
) processes, similar to those discussed for Assumption
1
.1.

As explained in Remark
1
, Assumption
1
.4 actually does not impose too many conditions on variables used to generate
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
. For instance, one may have weak factor signal through the factor loadings associated with
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
. If that is the case, one may add certain factor structure to
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
. In short, we focus on the current estimation approach in Section
2
.
We comment on Assumption
1
.5 as follows. This assumption only includes the factor loadings of the global factors, so it is similar to Assumption A of
Bai (2009)
, but with a lightly over specified number of factors that kicks in via
\mathbf{C}_{ij}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\circ})}+\mathbf{M}_{(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}_{j}^{\bullet})}
by the construction. This is indeed achievable as argued in
Moon and Weidner (2015)
. As we have not imposed too many conditions on the components of
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
up to this point, we state Assumption
1
.5 as it is. In a very extreme case in which
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}\equiv\mathbf{v}_{ijt}
, this condition reduces to
\min_{i,j}\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{V}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_{ij\centerdot}/T)
which can be realized under a set of additional conditions on
\mathbf{v}_{ijt}
and in connection with Lemma
B.2
in Appendix A below.

Assumption 2
.

1.

Suppose that
\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ijt}:=(\varepsilon_{ijt},\mathbf{v}_{ijt}^{\top})^{\top}\}
are independent of the other variables, and
E[\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ijt}]=\mathbf{0}
and
E[\varepsilon_{ijt}|\mathbf{v}_{ijt}]=0
, and for
\forall(i,j)
,
\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ijt}|t\in[T]\}
are strictly
\alpha
-mixing process with the
\alpha
-mixing coefficient

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}(t)=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{F}_{ij,-\infty}^{0},B\in\mathcal{F}_{ij,t}^{\infty}}|P(A)P(B)-P(AB)|

satisfying
\max_{i,j}\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}[\alpha_{ij}(t)]^{\kappa_{ij}/(4+\kappa_{ij})}<\infty
with
\kappa_{ij}>0
and
\max_{i,j}E\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ij1}\|^{2+\kappa_{ij}}<\infty
, where
\mathcal{F}_{ij,-\infty}^{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{ij,t}^{\infty}
are the
\sigma
-algebras generated by
\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ijs}:s\leq 0\}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ijs}:s\geq t\}
, respectively. Finally, there exits
\kappa^{*}\in(0,\min_{i,j}\kappa_{ij})
such that
LN/T^{1+\kappa^{*}/4}\to 0
.

2.

\max_{i,j}(\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ}\|^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet}\|^{2})=O_{P}(\log(LN))
.

3.

The minimum eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},ij}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}E[\mathbf{V}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_{ij\centerdot}]
is positive and uniformly bounded away from zero for all
i
and
j
.

The mixing condition of Assumption
2
.1 is necessary to derive Lemma
B.2
, which is then further required to derive Lemma
2
and the asymptotic distribution. The requirements about
\kappa_{ij}
’s impose conditions on the existence of some moments of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ijt}
, which can be easily fulfilled, e.g.,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{ijt}
follows a normal distribution. Assumption
2
.2 is standard.

2.3
Asymptotic Properties

Under the main assumptions, we are now ready to establish the consistency and asymptotic normality results.

Lemma 1
.

For
Step 1
, under Assumption
1
, as
(L,N,T)\to(\infty,\infty,\infty)
, we have

1.

\frac{1}{\sqrt{LN}}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot\centerdot}-\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}\|=o_{P}(1)
;

2.

\|\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}}-\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}}\|=O_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{LN}}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot\centerdot}-\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}\|+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L\wedge N\wedge T}}\right)
.

The establishment of Lemma
1
does not require an excessive number of conditions for the variables involved in (
2.1
). It shows that we can recover
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot\centerdot}
as a whole. As expected, the global factor
\mathbf{F}
can be estimated firstly by putting everything together.

As shown in (
1.4
) and (
2.12
), in order to recover
\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}
, we need to divide the data into different blocks as presented in the estimation procedure, so
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}
’s are automatically partitioned into blocks accordingly. As a consequence, we can no longer use the overall consistency of
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}
derived in Lemma
1
. To proceed, we further impose Assumption
2
, and summarize the results associated with
\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}
in Lemma
2
below.

Lemma 2
.

For
Step 1
, under Assumptions
1
and
2
, as
(L,N,T)\to(\infty,\infty,\infty)
,

1.

\max_{i,j}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}-\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}\|=o_{P}(1)
;

2.

\max_{i}\|\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}}-\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\circ}}\|=O_{P}(\max_{i,j}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}-\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}\|+\frac{\sqrt{\log(LN)}}{\sqrt{N\wedge T}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}+\frac{\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i\centerdot\centerdot}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{NT}}+\frac{\max_{i}\|\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ\top}\mathbf{F}\|_{2}}{T})
;

3.

\max_{j}\|\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}}-\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j}^{\bullet}}\|=O_{P}(\max_{i,j}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}-\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}\|+\frac{\sqrt{\log(LN)}}{\sqrt{L\wedge T}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}+\frac{\max_{j}\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\centerdot j\centerdot}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{LT}}+\frac{\max_{j}\|\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet\top}\mathbf{F}\|_{2}}{T})
.

Lemma
2
justifies the validity of our approach across all blocks involved in
Step 1
. With this, we have successfully recovered all key components of model (
2.2
), thus concluding our examination of
Step 1
in the estimation procedure.

Now, we proceed to Step 2, where we update the estimator for
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
. Additionally, we consider two mean group estimators to infer the average values of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
on two cross-sectional dimensions, respectively. For
\forall(i,j)
, define

\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i\centerdot}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij},\quad\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot j}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}.

To facilitate the theoretical development, we introduce some additional notation.
Let
\mathcal{R}_{ij}^{*}=(\mathcal{R}_{F},\mathcal{R}_{F,i}^{\circ},\mathcal{R}_{F,j}^{\bullet})
, where
\mathcal{R}_{F}=\sum_{ij}(\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ}+\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet})\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\centerdot\centerdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\centerdot\centerdot})^{-1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{F,i}^{\circ}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(\mathbf{F}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}+\mathbf{F}_{j}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet})\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ\top}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\centerdot}^{\circ\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\centerdot}^{\circ})^{-1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{F,j}^{\bullet}=\sum_{i=1}^{L}(\mathbf{F}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}+\mathbf{F}_{i}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ})\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet\top}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\centerdot j}^{\bullet\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\centerdot j}^{\bullet})^{-1}
.

Theorem 1
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. As
(L,N,T)\to(\infty,\infty,\infty)
,

(1)

for
\forall(i,j)
, if
\sqrt{T}/(L\wedge N)\to 0
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij})\to_{D}N(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},ij}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},\varepsilon,ij}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},ij}^{-1}),

where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},\varepsilon,ij}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}E[\mathbf{V}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij\centerdot}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_{ij\centerdot}]
.

(2)

for
\forall i
, if
\sqrt{T}/(L\wedge N)\to 0
and
\sqrt{N}/(L\wedge T)\to 0
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{NT}(\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i\centerdot}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i\centerdot}-\mathcal{B}_{i\centerdot})\to_{D}N(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{b},i\centerdot}),

where
\mathcal{B}_{i\centerdot}=\frac{1}{NT}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},ij}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{*\top}\mathcal{R}_{ij}^{*\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}}\mathcal{R}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}
is the bias term with the probability order of
O_{P}(\frac{1}{L\wedge N\wedge T})
, and

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{b},i\centerdot}=\lim_{N,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{NT}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},ij_{1}}^{-1}E[\mathbf{V}_{ij_{1}\centerdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij_{1}\centerdot}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{ij_{2}\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_{ij_{2}\centerdot}]\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},ij_{2}}^{-1}
.

(3)

for
\forall j
, if
\sqrt{T}/(L\wedge N)\to 0
and
\sqrt{L}/(N\wedge T)\to 0
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{LT}(\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot j}-\mathcal{B}_{\centerdot j})\to_{D}N(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{b},\centerdot j}),

where
\mathcal{B}_{\centerdot j}=\frac{1}{LT}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},ij}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{*\top}\mathcal{R}_{ij}^{*\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}}\mathcal{R}_{ij}^{*}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{*}
is the bias term with the probability order of
O_{P}(\frac{1}{L\wedge N\wedge T})
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{b},\centerdot j}=\lim_{L,T\to\infty}\frac{1}{LT}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{L}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},i_{1}j}^{-1}E[\mathbf{V}_{i_{1}j\centerdot}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i_{2}j\centerdot}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i_{2}j\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_{i_{2}j\centerdot}]\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},i_{2}j}^{-1}
.

Theorem
1
.(1) provides the asymptotic distribution for each
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}
. Meanwhile, Theorem
1
.(2-3) establish the asymptotic normality for the mean group estimators, which incorporate bias terms to achieve the optimal rates of convergence (
\sqrt{NT}
and
\sqrt{LT}
, respectively). The presence of bias terms arises due to the hierarchical factor structure, which can be addressed using conventional methods such as the analytical approach or the Jackknife procedure
(see
Chen et al., 2021
, for example)
. In Appendix
B.2
below, we propose a Jackknife method to remove the bias terms present in the theorem.

To conduct practical inference, it is essential to deal with the time series correlation involved in the term
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{v},\varepsilon,ij}
. Therefore, we now focus on Theorem
1
.(1) and then develop a new dependent wild bootstrap procedure by considerably extending
Gao et al. (2023)
for the standard time–series and cross-sectional panel data setting to the following setting where the double–index
(i,j)
involved can be both cross–sectional. For the second and third parts of Theorem
1
, the corresponding bootstrap procedure involves a two–step approach in each case. The first step is a bias correction procedure as discussed in Appendix
B.2
below, and the second step develops a bootstrap procedure for each case in almost the same way as proposed below.

Bootstrap Procedure:

(i).

Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{T})^{\top}
be an

m
-dependent time series for each bootstrap draw, and let the components of
\boldsymbol{\xi}
satisfy that

\displaystyle E[\xi_{t}]=0,\quad E|\xi_{t}|^{2}=1,\quad E|\xi_{t}|^{2+\kappa_{ij}/2}<\infty,\quad E[\xi_{t}\xi_{s}]=a\left(\frac{t-s}{m}\right),

where
m\to\infty
,
\kappa_{ij}
is a constant and defined in Assumption
2
, and
a(\cdot)
is a symmetric kernel defined on
[-1,1]
satisfying that
a(0)=1
and
K_{a}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}a(u)e^{-iux}du\geq 0
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii).

For
\forall(i,j)
, construct a new set of dependent
variables by
\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}^{*}=\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}}_{ij\centerdot}\circ\boldsymbol{\xi}
, where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}}_{ij\centerdot}=\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}-\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}
. Accordingly, the new estimate of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
is obtained using

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}^{*}=\left(\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{ij\centerdot}^{\top}\mathbf{M}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{ij}}\mathbf{Y}_{ij\centerdot}^{*},

(iii).

We repeat the above procedure
\mathcal{L}
times.

Theorem 2
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Assume further that
\frac{m}{\sqrt{Th}}\to 0
and
mT^{2/\underline{\kappa}_{ij}-1}\rightarrow 0
, where
\underline{\kappa}_{ij}=\min(\kappa^{\ast}_{ij},4)
,
\kappa^{\ast}_{ij}=1/(\frac{1}{2+\kappa_{ij}}+\frac{\kappa_{ij}}{2(4+\kappa_{ij})})
, and
\kappa_{ij}
is defined in Assumption
2
.
As
(L,N,T)\to(\infty,\infty,\infty)
, for
\forall(i,j)

\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\text{Pr}^{*}(\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}^{*}-\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij})\leq w)-\Pr(\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij})\leq w)\right|=o_{P}(1).

Theorem
2
provides a procedure to recover the asymptotic distribution for each given
(i,j)
. It is worth mentioning that the DWB method is initially introduced in
Shao (2010)
in the context of time series analysis, wherein a comprehensive comparison between the DWB and some existing bootstrap methods can be found.

2.4
Selection of the Numbers of Factors

To conclude our theoretical investigation, we provide a procedure to select the numbers of factors. For notational simplicity, we define
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}=(\ell_{1}^{\circ},\ldots,\ell_{L}^{\circ})^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}=(\ell_{1}^{\bullet},\ldots,\ell_{N}^{\bullet})^{\top}.
Thus, our goal is to estimate
\ell
,
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}
, and
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}
.

First, we emphasize that we can still get consistent estimation of
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}
by prescribing a
d_{\max}
to be the numbers of global, country and industry factors. As long as
d_{\max}\geq(\ell\vee(\max_{i}\ell_{i}^{\circ})\vee(\max_{i}\ell_{j}^{\bullet}))
, the estimation procedure of Section
2.1
, and the results developed in Section
2.2
still hold true. In connection with the estimation procedure of Section
2.1
, we provide the following steps to estimate
\ell
firstly, and then estimate the elements of
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}
sequentially.

Step 1*
Prescribe a
d_{\max}
to be the numbers of global, country and industry factors, and implement the estimation procedure of Section
2.1
to get
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}=(\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{11},\ldots,\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{1N},\ldots,\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{LN})^{\top}
.

Step 2*
Using
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}
, we calculate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
as in Section
2.1
, and estimate the number of global factors as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\ell}=\argmin_{0\leq s\leq d_{\max}}\Big\{\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{s+1}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{s}}\cdot I(\widehat{\lambda}_{s}\geq\omega)+I(\widehat{\lambda}_{s}<\omega)\Big\},

(2.15)

where
\omega=[\log(L\vee N\vee T)]^{-1}
,
\widehat{\lambda}_{0}=1
is a mock eigenvalue, and
\widehat{\lambda}_{s}
stands for the
s^{th}
largest eigenvalue of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. Record the eigenvectors corresponding to the largest
\widehat{\ell}
eigenvalues in
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
.

Step 3*
Having obtained
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}
, we sequentially estimate
\ell_{i}^{\circ}
’s and
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}
’s as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\ell}_{i}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\argmin_{0\leq s\leq d_{\max}}\Big\{\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{i,s+1}^{\circ}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{i,s}^{\circ}}\cdot I(\widehat{\lambda}_{i,s}^{\circ}\geq\omega)+I(\widehat{\lambda}_{i,s}^{\circ}<\omega)\Big\},

\displaystyle\widehat{\ell}_{j}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\argmin_{0\leq s\leq d_{\max}}\Big\{\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,s+1}^{\bullet}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{j,s}^{\bullet}}\cdot I(\widehat{\lambda}_{j,s}^{\bullet}\geq\omega)+I(\widehat{\lambda}_{j,s}^{\bullet}<\omega)\Big\},

(2.16)

where
\widehat{\lambda}_{i,0}^{\circ}=\widehat{\lambda}_{j,0}^{\bullet}=1
are mock eigenvalues, and
\widehat{\lambda}_{i,s}^{\circ}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{j,s}^{\bullet}
stand for the
s
-th largest eigenvalues of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{\circ}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j}^{\bullet}
respectively.

The estimators in (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) can be considered as extensions of
Lam and Yao (2012)
. However, as pointed out by
Lam and Yao (2012)
, it remains unresolved to bound the ratio associated with the eigenvalues which converge to 0 from below. To bypass this unresolved issue, we introduce a data driven tuning parameter
\omega
. The idea is that although it is challenging to study a ratio with a denominator converging to 0, we can discard this ratio and construct a V-shape curve by employing the indicator function in (
2.15
) and (
2.16
), respectively.

Theorem 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, as
(L,N,T)\to(\infty,\infty,\infty)
,
\Pr(\widehat{\ell}=\ell,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\bullet}=\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet})\to 1,
where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\circ}=(\widehat{\ell}_{1}^{\circ},\ldots,\widehat{\ell}_{L}^{\circ})^{\top}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\bullet}=(\widehat{\ell}_{1}^{\bullet},\ldots,\widehat{\ell}_{L}^{\bullet})^{\top}
.

Up to this point, we have successfully recovered all the unknown quantities as mentioned in the beginning of Section
2
. In the next section, we examine the above theoretical results using extensive simulations.

Remark 4
.

To conclude this section, we note that practically it is not guaranteed that each country (or industry) has the same number of individuals, so it brings certain unbalanceness to the data set. Formally,
\forall i\in[L]
, we may have
j\in[N_{i}]
, which is exactly the same as our empirical study of Section
4
. After carefully checking the proofs, we can see that as long as
\min_{i}N_{i}\to\infty
and
\frac{T}{\min_{i}N_{i}^{2}}\to 0
, all the aforementioned results are still valid with certain modifications.

3
Simulation Studies

In this section, we conduct extensive numerical studies to examine the theoretical findings. Specifically, we provide simulation studies, and the main data generating process (DGP) follows (
2.1
). In what follows, we provide details about each component involved.

To capture heterogeneity of the coefficients, we generate them by
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}=(0.5+i/L,0.5+j/N)^{\top}
for
(i,j)\in[L]\times[N]
, which implies
d=2
. For factors and loadings, we first generate the numbers of factors. Specifically, we let
\ell=2
, and let
\ell_{i}^{\circ}
and
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}
be randomly selected from the set
\{0,1,2\}
with equal probability attached to each possible outcome. The design is to show that we allow
\ell_{i}^{\circ}
and
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}
to be 0, which is meaningful for practical analysis. Therefore, for each generated dataset, only
\ell
is fixed. The values of
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}
vary at each simulation replication.

Sequentially, we generate factors and loadings, and let them be independently drawn from some normal distributions:
\mathbf{f}_{t}\sim N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{\ell})
,
\mathbf{f}_{it}^{\circ}\sim N(\mathbf{0},2\cdot\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{i}^{\circ}})
if
\ell_{i}^{\circ}>0
,
\mathbf{f}_{jt}^{\bullet}\sim N(\mathbf{0},2\cdot\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{j}^{\bullet}})
if
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}>0
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}\sim N(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{I}_{\ell})
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\circ}\sim N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{i}^{\circ}})
if
\ell_{i}^{\circ}>0
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{ij}^{\bullet}\sim N(-\mathbf{1},\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{j}^{\bullet}})
if
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}>0
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij,s}\sim N(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{I}_{\ell})
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij,s}^{\circ}\sim N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{i}^{\circ}})
if
\ell_{i}^{\circ}>0
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij,s}^{\bullet}\sim N(-\mathbf{1},\mathbf{I}_{\ell_{j}^{\bullet}})
if
\ell_{j}^{\bullet}>0
, where
s\in[d]
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij,s}
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij,s}^{\circ}
, and
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij,s}^{\bullet}
stand for the
s^{th}
columns of
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\circ}
, and
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij}^{\bullet}
respectively. We note that normal distributions are not really necessary here, as the choice of some other distributions doesn’t affect the finite-sample performance. To present the main steps for the simulation and save the space, we do not further explore alternative options.

We generate residuals as follows:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\centerdot\centerdot t}=\rho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\centerdot\centerdot t-1}+0.5\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\cdot N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{LN})^{\top},\quad\mathbf{v}_{\centerdot\centerdot t,s}=\rho_{v}\mathbf{v}_{\centerdot\centerdot t-1,s}+\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}^{1/2}\cdot N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{LN})^{\top},

where
\rho_{\varepsilon}=\rho_{v}=0.1
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}=\{0.2^{\|(i_{1},j_{1})-(i_{2},j_{2})\|}\}_{LN\times LN}
with
(i,j)\in[L]\times[N]
, and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\centerdot\centerdot t}=(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{11t},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1Nt},\ldots\ldots,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{LNt}),\quad\mathbf{v}_{\centerdot\centerdot t,s}=(\mathbf{v}_{11t,s},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1Nt,s},\ldots\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{LNt,s}),

in which
\mathbf{v}_{ijt,s}
is the
s^{th}
element of
\mathbf{v}_{ijt}
, and
s\in[d]
. Based on the above DGP, we have weak serial correlation and cross-sectional dependence among
(\varepsilon_{ijt},\mathbf{v}_{ijt}^{\top})^{\top}
.

Finally, we assemble
y_{ijt}
’s and
\mathbf{x}_{ijt}
’s as in (
2.1
). For each dataset, we carefully calculate all the unknown quantities following the numerical implementation of Appendix
B.1
.

To evaluate the performance of our proposed method, we repeat the above procedure
R=1000
times, and define several criteria. For notational simplicity, the subindex
k
always stands for the corresponding quantities obtained at the
k^{th}
replication in what follows. That said, we use root mean square errors (RMSE) to measure our estimates about
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot\centerdot}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}
:

\displaystyle\text{RMSE}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\frac{1}{LN}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{\centerdot\centerdot,k}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot\centerdot}\|^{2}\right\}^{1/2},

\displaystyle\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\|\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{k}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}_{k}}\|^{2}\right\}^{1/2},

\displaystyle\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\circ}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\|\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i,k}^{\circ}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}_{i,k}^{\circ}}\|^{2}\right\}^{1/2},

\displaystyle\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\|\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j,k}^{\bullet}}-\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}_{j,k}^{\bullet}}\|^{2}\right\}^{1/2},

(3.1)

where we let
\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{k}}=\mathbf{0}
if
\widehat{\ell}=0
, and similarly, we let
\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{i,k}^{\circ}}=\mathbf{0}
,
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}_{i,k}^{\circ}}=\mathbf{0}
,
\mathbf{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{j,k}^{\bullet}}=\mathbf{0}
, and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{F}_{j,k}^{\bullet}}=\mathbf{0}
if
\widehat{\ell}_{i,k}^{\circ}=0
,
\ell_{i,k}^{\circ}=0
,
\widehat{\ell}_{j,k}^{\bullet}=0
, and
\ell_{j,k}^{\bullet}=0
respectively. For the purpose of comparison, we estimate
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\centerdot\centerdot}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}
by assuming that
\ell
,
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}
, and
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}
are known, and calculate
\text{RMSE}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
,
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}}^{*}
,
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\circ}}^{*}
, and
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}}^{*}
as in (
3.1
).

To evaluate the estimation about the numbers of factors, we define the following rates:

\displaystyle\text{Rate}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=1}^{R}I(\widehat{\ell}_{k}=\ell),\quad\text{Rate}_{\ell}^{-}=\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=1}^{R}I(\widehat{\ell}_{k}<\ell),\quad\text{Rate}_{\ell}^{+}=\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=1}^{R}I(\widehat{\ell}_{k}>\ell),

\displaystyle\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{RL}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\sum_{i=1}^{L}I(\widehat{\ell}_{i,k}^{\circ}=\ell_{i,k}^{\circ}),\quad\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}}=\frac{1}{RN}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\sum_{j=1}^{N}I(\widehat{\ell}_{j,k}^{\bullet}=\ell_{j,k}^{\bullet}),

\displaystyle\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}}^{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{RL}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\sum_{i=1}^{L}I(\widehat{\ell}_{i,k}^{\circ}<\ell_{i,k}^{\circ}),\quad\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}}^{-}=\frac{1}{RN}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\sum_{j=1}^{N}I(\widehat{\ell}_{j,k}^{\bullet}<\ell_{j,k}^{\bullet}),

\displaystyle\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{RL}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\sum_{i=1}^{L}I(\widehat{\ell}_{i,k}^{\circ}>\ell_{i,k}^{\circ}),\quad\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}}^{+}=\frac{1}{RN}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\sum_{j=1}^{N}I(\widehat{\ell}_{j,k}^{\bullet}>\ell_{j,k}^{\bullet}).

(3.2)

Apparently,
\text{Rate}_{\ell}
,
\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}}
, and
\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}}
measure the percentages of correctly identifying the numbers of global, country and industry factors;
\text{Rate}_{\ell}^{-}
,
\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}}^{-}
, and
\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}}^{-}
measure the percentages of under estimation;
\text{Rate}_{\ell}^{+}
,
\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}}^{+}
, and
\text{Rate}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}}^{+}
measure the percentages of over estimation.

Finally, we evaluate the coverage rates (CR) of the bootstrap procedure. For
s\in[d]
,

\displaystyle\text{CR}=\frac{1}{dRLN}\sum_{s=1}^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{R}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{j=1}^{N}I(\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij,s,k}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij,s}\in\text{CI}_{ij,s,k})

(3.3)

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij,s}
stands for the
s^{th}
element of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{ij}
,
\text{CI}_{ij,s,k}
stands for the 95% confidence interval obtained from the bootstrap draws associated with
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij,s,k}^{*}-\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij,s,k}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij,s,k}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij,s,k}^{*}
respectively stand for the
s^{th}
elements of
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{b}}_{ij}^{*}
obtained at the
k^{th}
simulation replication.

We summarize results in Tables
1
,
2
, and
3
. As
i
and
j
are symmetric in a sense, we alter
N
and
T
only in the following tables, while keeping
L=60
for simplicity. We now discuss each table in details. In Table
1
, as expected, as either
N
or
T
increases, we have improved probability of correctly identifying
\ell
,
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\circ}
, and
\boldsymbol{\ell}^{\bullet}
. Even for the case
(L,N,T)=(60,60,60)
, we are still able to identify different numbers of factors with probabilities greater than 70%. In Table
2
, as expected,
\text{RMSE}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}
,
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}}^{*}
,
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\circ}}^{*}
, and
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}}^{*}
outperform
\text{RMSE}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
,
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}}
,
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\circ}}
, and
\text{RMSE}_{\mathbf{F}^{\bullet}}
respectively. However, the differences are not very significant, and gradually vanish as the sample size increases. This is not surprising, as we have better probabilities to identify different types of factors with large sample size. As a result, it is less likely to have misspecified factor structures as the sample size increases. Table
3
reports the coverage rates of the bootstrap procedure. Overall, the numbers are very close to the nominal rate 95%, and even for
T=60
. To conclude, as explained in Remark
2
, we emphasize that the above results show our method works well for the case with
L\asymp N\asymp T
.
